[2026-10-15T21:24:15.152Z] [USER] What do you think how we can improve this prompt?
[2026-10-15T21:24:37.440Z] [DOT] The prompt is: *"first update 15 pro max so i can see latest new version, then continue."*

A few things to tighten up:

**1. "15 pro max" → name the simulator explicitly**
Claude has to infer you mean the iPhone 15 Pro Max simulator. Say "the iPhone 15 Pro Max simulator" to remove ambiguity.

**2. "latest new version" is redundant and vague**
"Latest" and "new" say the same thing, and "version" is unclear — version of what? The app build? Say "rebuild and install the app" instead.

**3. "then continue" is the weakest part**
Continue *what*? Claude has to guess which thread of work to pick up. Name the exact next step — e.g. "then continue testing the golf flows" or "then continue with the wallet fixes."

**A tighter rewrite:**
> "Rebuild and install the app on the iPhone 15 Pro Max simulator, then continue testing the [specific flow] end to end."

The big win is replacing "continue" with the actual next task. In a long session, "continue" is where things drift.

[2026-10-16T00:08:31.642Z] [DOT] Here's the prompt for Antigravity — it's already on your clipboard:

---

Add a new native action to Dot called `typeText` that types a given string into whatever app currently has keyboard focus.

**Implementation approach:**

1. In `NativeActions.swift` (or a new `KeyboardActions.swift`), add a function:

```swift
func typeText(_ text: String) {
    let source = CGEventSource(stateID: .combinedSessionState)
    for char in text.unicodeScalars {
        let keyData = [
            CGKeyCode(char.value),
            char.value
        ]
        // Use CGEvent keyboard events
        let keyDown = CGEvent(keyboardEventSource: source, virtualKey: CGKeyCode(char.value), keyDown: true)
        let keyUp = CGEvent(keyboardEventSource: source, virtualKey: CGKeyCode(char.value), keyDown: false)
        keyDown?.post(tap: .cghidEventTap)
        keyUp?.post(tap: .cghidEventTap)
    }
}
```

Actually, the more robust approach is to use `CGEvent.keyboardSetUnicodeString` which handles any character correctly regardless of keyboard layout:

```swift
func typeText(_ text: String) {
    let source = CGEventSource(stateID: .combinedSessionState)
    let utf16 = Array(text.utf16)
    let keyDown = CGEvent(keyboardEventSource: source, virtualKey: 0, keyDown: true)
    let keyUp = CGEvent(keyboardEventSource: source, virtualKey: 0, keyDown: false)
    keyDown?.keyboardSetUnicodeString(stringLength: utf16.count, unicodeString: utf16)
    keyUp?.keyboardSetUnicodeString(stringLength: utf16.count, unicodeString: utf16)
    keyDown?.post(tap: .cghidEventTap)
    keyUp?.post(tap: .cghidEventTap)
}
```

2. Register it as a callable action in the engine so the LLM can invoke it with a `text` parameter.

3. Add it to the tool/function schema so the model knows it can call `typeText(text: "whatever")`.

4. Also add a `pressKey` action for special keys (Enter, Tab, Escape, arrow keys, Cmd shortcuts) using `CGEvent` with appropriate virtual key codes.

5. Make sure the app has Accessibility permission (AXIsProcessTrusted) — if not, prompt the user to grant it in System Settings > Privacy > Accessibility.

**Requirements:**
- Must work on the currently focused window of any app
- Must handle Unicode characters correctly
- Must be exposed as a tool the LLM can call
- Log a confirmation message like "Typed 'Continue' into the focused app"

---

[2026-10-16T00:33:20.152Z] [USER] What you think which one more useful?
[2026-10-16T00:33:32.139Z] [DOT] Honestly, **keyboard shortcuts** win for day-to-day usefulness. They're deterministic, work across every app, and don't break when a UI element moves. "Refresh," "new tab," "copy," "paste" — those are 80% of what people actually need.

Mouse control is cooler but more fragile. One pixel off and you click the wrong thing. It shines for specific cases like "click that button I can see," but it's harder to make reliable.

So I'd ship keyboard first, mouse second.
[2026-10-16T00:34:11.800Z] [USER] I mean here in YouTube
[2026-10-16T00:34:25.455Z] [DOT] For YouTube specifically, **keyboard shortcuts** are way more useful. YouTube already has great built-in shortcuts — space to pause, J/K/L to skip, F for fullscreen, M to mute, arrows to seek. So one prompt like "pause" or "skip 10 seconds" and I can control your video hands-free.

Mouse control would be nice for "click that video" but you'd just as easily click it yourself.
[2026-10-16T00:34:36.624Z] [USER] look to my screen

Once a video is playing, keyboard shortcuts take over and become the star.

So honestly: **both together** make YouTube fully hands-free. But if forced to pick one for *browsing*, it's mouse.

Self-Healing Rule (snapshot + auto-rollback):
1. Before any self-modification, create a timestamped snapshot of the runtime files being changed (git commit, or copy to .dot-backups/).
2. Apply the change.
3. Run a built-in self-test (compile + smoke test) after the change.
4. If the self-test fails, automatically restore the last known-good snapshot and report exactly what broke.
5. If it succeeds, keep the change and log it.
6. Never apply multiple risky changes in one pass — one change, one test, one rollback point.
7. Keep the 10 most recent snapshots; prune older ones automatically.
EOF" | OUTCOME: (Exit code 0) | STATUS: SUCCESS

One quick thought while it works: the "covers content while moving" symptom usually means the move animation is sliding the window *over* the target UI instead of moving *out of the way first*. The fix is often to reposition to an empty region (corner/edge) before animating, rather than interpolating across the screen.
